The page karbytes_primary_values ranks four things that karbytes gives priority when deciding how to use its cognitive resources. Nature comes first, followed by consciousness, logic, and comfort. Each term answers a different question. Nature describes what reality encompasses. Consciousness describes how a portion of that reality can experience something. Logic describes the consistency that can be discerned in interactions within nature. Comfort describes how pleasure and pain figure in experience and attention. The ranking is explicitly karbytes’ own, and the numbers indicate relative priority from one through four.

The page on Karbytes For Life Blog gives September 25, 2025 as its last-update date and designates its contents as public domain. It identifies itself as a revision of a version dated June 13, 2025. That earlier version is available as a separate archived page, with its HTML source preserved in Karlina Object Extension Pack 49. I compared the current page with the archived June text, which agrees with its linked HTML source after whitespace is normalized. The June date identifies the version preserved in the archive. The archive page’s own footer also gives September 25 as its last-update date.

Nature, the highest priority, is defined as the single set encompassing all phenomena, all noumena, and two forms of pure nothingness. The page calls these non-generative nothingness and generative nothingness and relates them to zero and infinity, respectively. The linked NATURE document explains the contrast in terms of what can coexist with each form. What it now calls the exclusive void is absolute absence everywhere, extending to both phenomena and noumena. What it calls the nonexclusive void is a featureless substrate that can coexist with and permeate them. The names used in that linked document describe coexistence and leave the question of causal origin open. Zero corresponds to the case in which nothing exists or could exist. Infinity corresponds to the case in which infinitely many things exist or could exist.

The same linked document gives phenomena and noumena a meaning relative to a frame of reference. A phenomenon has distinguishable features that a frame renders and observes. Something directly observed by one frame can remain a noumenon to another frame that is situated elsewhere or has no direct access to it. The second frame can still have a thought about the thing, and that thought can itself be experienced as a phenomenon. This makes the scope of the first value much wider than the contents of karbytes’ own experience. Nature includes what any particular observer encounters, what is outside that observer’s immediate awareness, and the background in which the distinction is made.

Consciousness, ranked second, is the capacity of an allocation of nature to be aware of phenomena or noumena by rendering subjective qualia. The examples in the values page are sensory information and abstract thoughts. Its use of qualia therefore includes both what it is like to receive a sensory impression and what it is like to entertain an idea. The linked NATURE document illustrates this distinction between experience and description through color. A wavelength can be specified as a number, such as 700 nanometers, while redness is the experienced quality in the example. The measurable quantity and the subjective appearance describe different aspects of the event.

The consciousness entry also distinguishes how much an agent or frame of reference can render from what kinds of experience it can render. A difference in capacity can concern the amount of experiential content, the kinds available, or both. The term information-processing agent points to the project’s AGENCY page. That document describes an agent as a relatively finite partition of nature that renders perceptions, makes selections among possible behaviors, and carries out decisions in pursuit of goals. The values page draws on the part of that account concerned with rendering experience. Nature is the encompassing set, while a conscious agent has a particular range of experience within it.

Logic, ranked third, is the interaction of multiple components of nature in a consistent and apparently rule-based manner. The wording applies to interactions broadly and singles out abstract entities, especially symbols and quantities, as a setting in which the consistency is particularly apparent. The qualification “apparently” is part of the definition. What karbytes gives priority to is the recognizable order in how components relate to one another. The link on quantities leads to NUMBERS, where a number is defined as information representing one finite quantity. This gives a specific example of the abstract material the logic entry has in mind. A quantity can be represented by a symbol, and relations among such representations can exhibit the consistency the page calls logic.

Comfort, the fourth priority, is defined through the perceived relation of pleasure to pain. The fuller formulation involves pleasure being more prominent in experience and receiving greater priority than pain. The entry also offers a simpler formulation in which comfort consists in minimizing pain. The first formulation includes what stands out to the person and what the person gives priority to. The second allows a reduction in pain itself to count as comfort. Fourth place gives comfort the lowest rank within this particular set of priorities, while retaining it as one of the things karbytes explicitly values.

The archived June version puts the ranking in a broader frame. It describes the values as priorities for karbytes’ existence, first-person experience, and conduct. It ranks truth first, defining it as the way everything is actually configured within a singular and encompassing reality. Autonomy comes second and concerns an information-processing agent’s capacity to set and achieve its own goals. Innovation comes third and concerns changes that are relatively novel and help a system survive or help the agents using it reach their goals more effectively. Comfort comes fourth and describes a sentient agent experiencing a degree of pain it judges tolerable, especially while it also experiences pleasure. Kindness comes fifth and concerns behavior that tries to increase the comfort experienced by all sentient information-processing agents. The archived page identifies that five-value list as itself a revision of material from a June 13 journal entry.

The September version changes both the list and the purpose given for ranking it. Its introduction focuses on how karbytes allocates cognitive resources. Nature, consciousness, and logic occupy the first three positions formerly occupied by truth, autonomy, and innovation. There is continuity between the two definitions at the top. The older truth entry treats reality as a singular whole, and the newer nature entry retains that encompassing scope while naming phenomena, noumena, and forms of nothingness within it. Comfort is the only label retained in both lists. Its number stays at four, but it becomes the final entry, and its definition moves from tolerable pain in the presence of pleasure to the relative prominence and priority of pleasure or the minimization of pain. Kindness appears as a separate value in the June list. The September page presents a four-value ranking without that separate entry.

KARBYTES_RULES_FOR_ITSELF links to this page when it refers to authentic goals. In that companion, satisfaction is a person’s judgment that its time is being put to good use in service of such goals. The Primary Values page gives karbytes’ own ranking of what merits that attention. It also gives comfort a more specific role than satisfaction has in the companion. Comfort concerns the experience and priority of pleasure and pain. Satisfaction concerns whether the person regards the use of its time as serving what genuinely matters to it. In the September ranking, the first claim on karbytes’ attention is the whole of nature, followed by the capacity to experience it, the consistency discernible within it, and the qualities of experience that make it more comfortable.

The page karbytes_ontology outlines karbytes’ assumptions about what constitutes reality and how it behaves. It begins with the limits of what a frame of reference can experience, then considers how an agent pursues its goals, how pleasure and pain affect its behavior, and how recurring patterns make prediction possible. It closes by distinguishing physical laws, which it describes as assumptions about the behavior of matter and energy, from mathematical relations, which it treats as immutable. The entries give the terms specific meanings within karbytes’ framework.

The page on Karbytes For Life Blog gives September 28, 2025 as its last-update date and designates its contents as public domain. It identifies karbytes_primary_values as a companion and points to an archived September 2 version, whose HTML source is preserved in Karlina Object Extension Pack 49. I compared the two editions and checked the archived page against its linked HTML. Their text agrees after whitespace is normalized. The archived page distinguishes the September 2 version it reproduces from its own September 27 last-update date. It also identifies a still earlier version dated July 31.

The first entry defines solipsism, with the heading spelled SOLIPSIM on the page. The defining condition is that a frame of reference is aware only of what it contains. The page narrows this to the qualia available to that frame in what it experiences as its present moment. Awareness is therefore situated twice over. It belongs to a particular frame, and what is available within that frame belongs to its particular present. The definition concerns the relationship between awareness and its contents. Later entries can still refer to several agents, each with its own field of awareness.

The page uses R and R(t) to distinguish the whole history of a frame of reference from what the frame renders at one time. The variable t marks a discrete unit of time as elapsed according to R. R(t) represents the frame’s contents at the corresponding point in its timeline. The full R is described as the sum of all such instances, with the timeline providing the linear, chronological order of every quale the frame ever renders. The distinction makes room for a frame to have a history much larger than what it experiences at any one instant. A recollection available now would belong to the contents of the present state, while the original observation would occupy its own position in the history.

Selfishness extends the same account from awareness to concern and action. The page defines it as an inevitable tendency for an information-processing agent to concern itself with what transpires within its own field of awareness. It develops this idea through an agent’s ongoing pursuit of goals. The agent has a conceptual picture of its circumstances, considers possible actions, implements a decision, and receives sensory or computational feedback. The outcome matters to the agent through that feedback and through its judgment of whether the action is helping it attain its goals. Karbytes places the agent’s understanding of a situation, the decision it makes, and its assessment of the result within the same field.

The passage on altruism separates whom an action helps from the goal for which the acting agent undertakes it. In the example, agent X assists agent Y in attaining Y’s own goals. That assistance qualifies as altruistic behavior in the example because of what it does for Y. Karbytes argues that X still has to conceive and carry out the assistance primarily in service of X’s own goals. Helping Y can itself be among those goals. The claim concerns the standpoint and purpose from which X acts, even when the action enables Y to achieve something Y wants. This is how the page fits assistance to another person into its account of selfishness.

Hedonism is stated more briefly. It is the general tendency of a sentient information-processing agent to avoid experiencing pain and to seek pleasure. The two directions matter separately. An agent can act to escape or reduce an unpleasant experience, and it can act to bring about an enjoyable one. The current entry describes a tendency specific to sentient agents, whereas the preceding definition of selfishness is expressed as applying to any information-processing agent. In the framework, selfishness concerns whose field and goals organize the action. Hedonism identifies two kinds of experience that can give the action direction.

Determinism, in the September 28 edition, concerns the predictability of what an agent observes and remembers observing. An agent can predict the rendering of some qualia to a meaningful degree of accuracy because those qualia appear to be individual instances of recurring patterns. Each occurrence can be distinct while exhibiting associations that have appeared before. The consistency of those associations makes earlier observations informative about later ones. Karbytes locates the definition in the experience of the predicting agent. The agent encounters particular events, remembers some of them, and uses the regularities it can discern to anticipate what will be rendered next.

The following paragraph describes the laws of physics as assumptions about how discrete, distinct allocations of matter and energy interact and evolve. That evolution is considered as time elapses according to the observing frames of reference. The wording places physical law alongside the preceding account of prediction. A frame encounters patterns in how events unfold, and a proposed physical law describes how the material systems involved are expected to behave. The final paragraph gives mathematics a different status. It treats mathematical rules as immutable logical relations among numbers, including the relation of a number to itself. The example pairs 1 = 1 with 1 = 2 − 1. The page presents them as equivalent expressions of a fixed relation, with the right-hand side of the second expression resolving to the same value.

The archived September 2 version makes the changes in emphasis visible. Its introduction calls its contents statements about how reality works and directs readers to the Primary Values page for the priorities that constrain karbytes’ decisions. It gives hedonism a broader treatment than the September 28 version. It describes certain sufficiently complex agents as continually trying to minimize suffering and maximize pleasure. It also proposes hedonism as a basic heuristic for setting priorities. In that account, suffering can include subjective pain and damage to essential hardware or software. Pleasure can include the experience of reward, especially from reaching a goal, and the strengthening of the systems the agent depends on. The current definition concentrates on the tendency to seek pleasure and avoid pain as experiences.

The archive also gives a substantial account of compatibilism. It describes free will or agency as emerging from deterministic factors within an agent’s mental model of reality. In that model, the experience of choosing can obscure some of the unconscious and deterministic processes that underlie the behavior. The degree of agency depends on the agent’s hardware, internal software, and external environment. The archive describes a continuum whose lower endpoint is illustrated by a machine without an internal representation of itself or its environment. Its upper endpoint is a hypothetical omniscient and omnipotent being able to do anything. Between those endpoints, the agent’s sense of freedom can vary with the choices it can formulate and what it can accomplish.

To make that variation explicit, the earlier version proposes a function for the degree of agency in a particular decision. It adds the number of distinct options the agent can mentally generate before choosing one to the agent’s cumulative rate of success at attaining its goals up to that decision. One term concerns the options available at the moment of choice. The other concerns the record of what the agent has been able to achieve through its actions. The same archived passage defines determinism through the causal relationship between successive events and indeterminism through events without prior causes. The September 28 version puts the emphasis on a different aspect of determinism, the extent to which an observer can predict recurring patterns. The explicit compatibilist model and its proposed measure of agency belong to the archived edition.

The earlier mathematics entry makes a broader claim about the reach of mathematical relations. It describes them as true throughout space, time, matter, energy, and even pure nothingness. It places mathematics in a realm of pure potential independent of physical phenomena, while also giving it a role in the precise description of observed physical patterns. The archive characterizes physical laws as well-established yet open to challenge. The current version retains the immutability of mathematical relations and expresses it through a simple numerical identity, with physical laws discussed separately as assumptions about observed systems.

The companion Primary Values page gives a personal ranking of nature, consciousness, logic, and comfort. Those priorities connect to the ontology without duplicating its definitions. Nature is the encompassing reality within which frames and agents occur. Consciousness concerns the capacity to render the experience whose limits the solipsism entry describes. Logic concerns the consistency that makes patterns recognizable and underlies the final treatment of mathematics. Comfort concerns pleasure and pain as experienced and prioritized, the same two kinds of experience that the hedonism entry treats as influences on behavior. The ontology connects these ideas at the scale of an agent’s experience. An agent has a present field of awareness, acts through the goals and feedback available within that field, and can use patterns remembered from earlier experience to anticipate some of what comes next.

BEST_PRACTICES_FOR_USING_THE_WEB is karbytes’ guide to building a personal home on the World Wide Web. It recommends making a website, regularly preserving its pages and files, and using social media to find people and share what one publishes. The three practices serve different purposes. The website gives a person a place to organize memories, interests, and knowledge. Archiving gives that work a chance to survive damage or deletion. Social platforms give it a way to reach readers and become part of conversations. The page was last updated on November 11, 2023, and designates its contents as public domain.

The first practice is to create a free site on the hosted WordPress service. Karbytes recommends the Intergalactic theme because of its streamlined layout, high-contrast colors, and sans-serif type. Navigation matters as much as appearance. In the setup the article describes, a Menu button sits in the upper-right corner of every page. Opening it reveals an alphabetical list of the site’s published pages. This gives readers a consistent way to move through a site that might contain stories, pictures, links to favorite places on the web, and pages about different interests.

Karbytes uses its two personal sites, Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog, as examples. Both use Intergalactic. The article gives their author two reasons for keeping them. One is to store cherished memories in a place karbytes hopes to revisit for the rest of its life. The other is to share interests and knowledge with anyone who has unrestricted access to the surface web. The same page can therefore serve as a personal record and a public resource. The website gives those purposes a common location, with a menu through which either the author or a visitor can find the material again.

The second practice is to save each page to the Wayback Machine on a recurring basis. Karbytes describes archived copies as a way to recover pages after the live site has been altered, damaged, or deleted, whether by its author or by somebody else. A saved version can be looked up and used to reconstruct the lost page. The article expresses considerable confidence in the long-term value of these copies, while reporting several ways an individual save can fail. A capture can appear to have succeeded and later be missing. A save can also capture an earlier version when the intention was to preserve the latest one. For the latter case, the article gives a forty-five-minute wait before trying the same page again. These experiences explain why karbytes recommends making repeated saves over time. Its own stated routine was to save every page of its personal websites at least once every three months, with the intention of continuing for life.

The article reports a daily limit of ten saves for the same address and a lower limit of two for certain resources, such as raw MP4 files in public GitHub repositories. Those are the figures given in the 2023 account. Karbytes also describes what a collection of archived pages can offer when the captures are usable together. In its account, following an internal link from a saved page opens the most recent archived copy of the target. If all the site’s pages have been saved, a visitor can approximate the experience of moving through the original site using the Wayback Machine, an Internet connection, a browser, and a computer or smartphone.

A separate problem arises when a page and the media displayed inside it are captured differently. The article says a saved page can be available while embedded elements are missing. It singles out large images, playable videos, and multimedia widgets as prone to this problem. The workaround karbytes uses is to store image files, videos, and web page source files in at least one public GitHub repository. Each file can then be submitted separately using the address of its raw version. The page recommends listing those direct file references on the WordPress site as well. In that arrangement, WordPress provides a readable page and a navigable index, GitHub holds the individual artifacts, and the Wayback Machine can capture the page and the underlying files separately. The article points to KARBYTES_CODE_CONTINUES and the raw-file macro-directory in connection with that practice.

The third practice is to create and use social media accounts. Karbytes describes these services as places to follow topics of interest, take part in public or private discussions, and share ideas, images, and links. It recommends Twitter and Minds for regular use because, in the author’s experience, they allow public material to be seen by people without accounts on those services. It also mentions using LinkedIn, Patreon, Instagram, and Facebook, with Facebook used only rarely. Karbytes says its experience of Facebook is that it is more oriented toward sharing with friends and family, and it gives content censorship as another reason it prefers other services for reaching a wider audience.

Instagram receives a separate discussion. Karbytes finds it an enjoyable source of news and sees it as especially valuable to visual artists because of its emphasis on images. The article describes the profile layout in detail. Photographs and video posts appear as equal-sized clickable squares in three columns. The newest post occupies the upper-left position, and the sequence runs through to the oldest at the lower right. This gives a visitor a way to survey a body of visual work before opening individual posts. Karbytes says the home page of either personal website gives readers a way to find its profiles on Twitter, Minds, LinkedIn, Instagram, and Patreon. The closing invitation specifically names Twitter, Minds, Instagram, and LinkedIn as places to follow new material, including news articles, videos, and personal blog posts, or to respond through comments and direct messages.

Beyond setting up accounts and saving files, the article encourages readers to spend time exploring whatever interests them. It describes Wikipedia as a volunteer-built encyclopedia covering many subjects, YouTube as a collection of videos made by people ranging from ordinary contributors to experts, and Reddit as a gathering place for many topic-specific forums. The point of these examples is to describe how karbytes became comfortable using the web. Following one’s curiosity gives repeated practice finding resources, moving between sites, and discovering what kinds of participation are enjoyable.

The closing widens the time horizon. Karbytes imagines a future Internet extending from Earth to major space stations and Mars. It expects the amount of data on the web and the number of people using it to grow as long as humanity remains sufficiently large and cooperative. It also speculates that advances in medicine, such as replacing damaged tissue with tissue grown from stem cells, could someday extend lifespans indefinitely and bring the death rate close to zero. That speculation echoes the earlier aside about archiving the sites for the rest of its life. The immediate practice is more concrete. A person can start with a site worth returning to, keep copies of the pages and the files they depend on, and use the wider web to find readers, conversations, and new things to learn.

The journal names karbytes as its author and designates the content as public domain. The web presentation turns some of the copied text into hyperlinks. I examined the linked source files, both still images, and selected frames from the demonstration video. The account of the network setup comes from the journal.

Karbytes says the project began with a search for free dark-web hosting. The services it found advertised as free were offline, and the available alternatives charged a fee. Since the intended site was a small demonstration of what an onion page could be, karbytes wanted to avoid paying a hosting provider. It also found the idea of serving the page from its own computer more engaging. The journal credits ChatGPT-4o with producing the instructions karbytes consulted and says the version reproduced on the page was abridged for brevity.

The Apache part of those instructions begins with refreshing the package information and installing Apache on a Debian- or Ubuntu-based system. It then opens the server’s document directory, /var/www/html, in the Nautilus file manager with administrative privileges so the custom files can be placed there. The instructions edit Apache’s 000-default.conf in the sites-available directory. The example keeps the document root at /var/www/html and sets the DirectoryIndex for the port-80 virtual host to the chosen HTML file. Restarting Apache applies the configuration. In karbytes’ setup, that change determines which page the server returns when a browser asks for the site’s root.

The Tor part installs the Tor package and edits /etc/tor/torrc. The example assigns the service directory /var/lib/tor/hidden_service/ and forwards the onion service’s port 80 to Apache on port 80 at the machine’s loopback address. After restarting Tor, the instructions read the generated onion hostname from the hostname file in that service directory. Apache and Tor have distinct roles in the arrangement. Apache supplies the page and its files. Tor supplies the route by which a Tor-capable client can request them. The journal makes remote availability conditional on the laptop being on and connected, with both services running and the correct files in place.

The October 1 photograph preserves the initial result before Apache’s default page was replaced. It shows an Android phone held horizontally against a dark background. Karbytes considered two applications for the custom page. It first tried BEATS_MACHINE, which depended on the Web Audio API, and reports encountering a restriction in its Tor browser setup. The journal includes the feature check provided by ChatGPT-4o. It looks for either AudioContext or webkitAudioContext, creates an audio context when one is available, and otherwise displays an alert explaining that sound generation will fail. Karbytes ultimately chose PI_APPROXIMATION as the interactive demonstration. The new page used a canvas and numerical readouts to make its activity visible while it ran.

The custom HTML welcomes the visitor to karbytes’ first onion page and dates its availability notice to October 1. It links back to the surface-web account of how it was created, identifies Karlina Object, Karbytes For Life Blog, and the project’s GitHub presence, and points to the separate surface-web tutorial for PI_APPROXIMATION. The linked source loads pi_approximation.js and karbytes_aesthetic.css. In the screenshot, an orange title appears on black above a pale green introduction. Cyan highlights the availability notice, yellow marks the description of the application, and green text sets out the mathematical relations. The canvas, counters, and timestamped event log occupy the lower part of the page. The counters are cyan, while the log is green, and both sit in panels outlined in magenta.

PI_APPROXIMATION is a Monte Carlo simulation. Its white canvas is a square 400 pixels on each side, with horizontal and vertical lines marking the center. The intended run lasts 3,600 seconds and plots one randomly positioned dart per second. The JavaScript generates a pair of signed integer coordinates, converts the pair to a canvas position, and tests its distance from the center. A dart at or within 200 pixels of the center is red. A dart farther away is blue. The red points therefore accumulate within the inscribed circle, while the blue points occupy the parts of the square beyond its boundary. The app divides the red count by the total count and multiplies the result by four. The reason is geometric. A circle of radius 200 occupies pi divided by four of the area of a square with side length 400. The fraction of throws landing in the circle estimates that area ratio. The displayed estimate can change with each new throw, alongside the countdown and the two color counts.

The local Firefox test comes after the change to Apache’s configuration. Karbytes says it refreshed Firefox at the laptop’s localhost address and saw that the server was now returning unlisted_web_page_karbytes_first_onion_web_page.html. The journal gives a screenshot restriction it encountered in Tor as the reason for using Firefox to record this view. The local request checks the file Apache delivers on the server machine. The photograph and video made with the Android phone document access through Tor from a separate device.

The October 2 Firefox screenshot captures a run well under way. The square contains a dense scattering of red dots through the center and blue dots concentrated toward the corners. The readout shows 2,324 seconds remaining, 1,008 red throws, 268 blue throws, and an estimate of 3.1598746081504703. The counts total 1,276, corresponding to the 1,276 seconds that have elapsed in the app’s countdown. Below them, the event log records initialization and the start of the simulation using timestamps measured in milliseconds from the Unix epoch. The still captures both the custom page Apache served and a populated state produced by its script.

The remote demonstration runs about two minutes and thirteen seconds. The phone is repeatedly held close to the camera in a dim room. Its bright screen is sometimes partly obscured by glare or turned away while karbytes operates it. The canvas has only a sparse scattering of points at that stage.
